[result: ### Motion hygiene — every one of these shipped as a bug first

- **A pulse must die down, never stop dead.** Any oscillating emphasis (a throb,
  a breathe, a sweep) gets an envelope that ramps in AND out. Cutting the
  oscillation at the end of its window snaps the element back to size and reads
  as a glitch. `abs(sin(e·hz)) · min(1, e/0.28) · m]

## ASSISTANT
Everything from this session, marked against what's already in `comic-animation.md`.

## Motion

1. **NEW — Animate transforms, never box dimensions.** Animating `width`, `height`, `top` or border-widths makes the renderer re-snap hard edges to whole pixels every frame. A 1px toggle on a 250px black bar is the arrow flicker. Draw once at a fixed size; move it with `scaleX/scaleY/translate` + `willChange: transform`.
2. **NEW — Diff lossless frames before blaming the encoder.** Render `--sequence --frames=A-B` and measure frame-to-frame change. *Alternating* high/low = pixel snapping. A smooth curve = fine. This is what separated the animation bug from the h.264 guess.
3. **NEW — Pulse rate ceiling.** 5.5 rad/s is a 0.57s cycle, nearly two throbs a second; on several synced elements that's a strobe. ~3.4 rad/s (about one a second) reads as a heartbeat. Amplitude was never the problem.
4. **NEW — Entrances form, they don't arrive.** A queue enters from one point, single file, at a shared pace, and fills from the front. Four things sliding in on parallel tracks is not a queue; four things rising together is not one either.
5. **NEW — Smootherstep for anything entering.** Zero velocity at both ends. Plain ease-out starts at full speed, so figures look dealt out like cards.
6. **NEW — Stagger tight enough to overlap.** 0.24s apart reads as four separate arrivals; 0.13s reads as one movement.
7. **NEW — Gait bobs must have no cusps.** `abs(sin)` has a V at every footfall, which at 30fps is a mechanical hitch. Use a signed sine inside a window that opens and closes on its own.
8. **NEW — The no-overlap rule applies during motion, not just at rest.** Verify numerically across the whole entrance that nobody closes below the resting pitch mid-walk.
9. **EXTENDS "derive positions from neighbours"** — derive across *representations*: the dot's centre comes from the figure's head centre (`y + 26·k`), so one becomes the other in place.

## Explainers

10. **NEW — The metaphor's shape must match the concept's shape.** Doors are about permission. Dependencies are about ordering. A gating metaphor cannot carry a dependency lesson, which is exactly why it read as subagents.
11. **NEW — A label can carry the edge.** `WAITING ON API` instead of `PLEASE WAIT`: same pixels, same position, but one is a queue sign and the other is a dependency attached to the thing doing the waiting.
12. **NEW — Weight follows the lesson.** Whatever gets the biggest animation is what gets remembered. If the parallel finish out-animates the dependency, the audience learns parallelism.
13. **NEW — Transform in place, then move.** Entities become the diagram *where they stand*; only then does the one that must move, move. Cross-fading between two different layouts reads as a cut to a different picture.
14. **NEW — Don't spend the payoff in the setup.** The AI explaining parallelism in panel 11 turns the explainer into a recap.
15. **NEW — Reuse the vocabulary you just taught.** "You gave me four dots and no arrows" beats restating the same idea in different words.
16. **NEW — A static screen cannot hold a long line.** Put the long half on the shot that moves. 6.8s on a motionless logo was the complaint; the fix was redistribution, not cutting.

## SFX

17. **NEW — A cue under speech in the same register is inaudible however loud you make it.** Check the VO envelope first and move the cue into a gap. The thud was firing correctly on the frame the AI started talking.
18. **NEW — Realistic is not audible.** 82% of the thud's energy was below 80Hz; laptop and phone speakers roll off around 150Hz. Put the body at 160–320Hz.
19. **NEW — The onset transient is what gives a cue definition.** Lead the body by ~3ms with a bright crack; aim for ~40% of the first 12ms above 1200Hz.
20. **NEW — Verify a cue in the mix, not in the file.** Measure band energy in the render at the cue time against the floor either side.

## VO

21. **NEW — Transcribe every delivered take before using it.** ElevenLabs silently changed "one at a time" to "at the same time", which inverts the episode.
22. **NEW — When a transcript is contested, test acoustically against a control clip.** Count fricative bursts: a known-good "one at a time" has one burst (the /t/); "at the same time" has an extra sustained 80ms /s/. Two models can share a bias; a control can't.
23. **NEW — Audit every VO asset's level against the median before shipping.** Ken's off-screen line was 7.7dB under with its peak 14dB down. One command catches it.
24. **NEW — Match a new take's level before cutting it.** The David take arrived 3.5dB under the other AI lines.
25. **NEW — A sentence that spans a cut must come from one take, split.** Two generations put different breath and room tone either side of the join, which is the seam the structure exists to hide.
26. **EXTENDS "pace with pauses"** — trimming a take's *internal* pause is fair game (1.56s → 0.8s after "it's 9 am"), and it's a judgment call worth flagging rather than doing silently.

## Ambience beds

27. **NEW — A baked bed is length-specific. Regenerate it on every timing change.** The film outgrew it and ran the last six seconds on digital silence.
28. **NEW — Never trust a stored detection map; re-derive from the audio.** `amb_spikes.json` lists instantaneous clicks. The printer is a sustained ten-second run. Every cut you've heard had it.
29. **NEW — Detect the printer by HF-to-LF ratio, not absolute HF.** That ratio is what separates a printer from a voice or a chair.
30. **NEW — Cut aggressively, verify at what's audible.** Two thresholds: detect at +3.5dB, verify at +6dB.
31. **NEW — Repeats are fine where nothing plays.** Restart the material at the night silence. Your idea, and it's what made a printer-free bed possible at all.
32. **NEW — Loop by rendering long and crossfading the overrun over the opening**, so t=0 continues the last sample instead of butting against it.
33. **NEW — Level the whole stream BEFORE the loop crossfade.** Run afterwards it gives the two ends different gains and the seam steps even though the waveform is continuous.
34. **NEW — Waveform continuity is not enough; the two ends must match in level.** Choose which window opens the second pass on that basis.
35. **NEW — Macro-level on a ~6s window.** Long enough to leave voices, keyboards and footsteps alone; short enough to catch a stretch that just sits quieter.
36. **NEW — Don't splice around a defect you can lift.** The 6s lull was fixed by levelling, not by a join.
37. **NEW — Keep the recording's worst stretch away from the loop point.** Score slice choice with head and tail weighted hard.
38. **NEW — Verify the night as true silence, not just quiet**, and exclude it from any ratio-based check (a ratio on silence is meaningless and reads as a false positive).

## Story and script

39. **NEW — A loop needs a sentence that spans the cut, not a repeat.** The last panel is frame 1's own plate, so the picture is identical and only the voice moves.
40. **NEW — Set the want up top.** A deadline stated as a request registers; a deadline agreed to with a nod doesn't. Prashasti's and Anandhi's notes were the same note.
41. **NEW — Keep the AI blameless.** If it promises speed and then fails, the ending lands on the tool instead of the character.
42. **NEW — Two reviewers converging independently is the strongest signal in a feedback thread**, stronger than any single sharp note.
43. **NEW — A question asked in the last beat needs an answer, or the character reads as lost rather than trapped.**

## Captions

44. **NEW — Split a caption on the speaker's own pause**, especially when the last word is the joke. A held block puts `but-` on screen five seconds before he says it and spoils the cliffhanger.

## Process and tooling

45. **NEW — Build scripts take the timeline as arguments and print their own proof.** `ambience.py` prints length, loop seam, night floor, day flatness, printer count. A generator that can't prove itself gets trusted when it shouldn't be.
46. **NEW — Keep `_prev/` backups of every replaced audio asset.** They're the only copies of pre-edit VO.
47. **NEW — Use automatic port selection for the studio.** A fixed port collided with an in-flight render and killed it.
48. **NEW — The Drive connector can't upload video.** Inline base64 only, so anything over a few hundred KB has to be dragged. Rename locally to the convention first.
49. **NEW — Drive naming convention:** `EP00N <slug> — 9x16 — NNs (what's distinctive).mp4`, matching EP001.
50. **NEW — Upload as a new version (Manage versions), not a second file**, so shared links stay valid.

---

Two things already in the file that this episode re-confirmed rather than changed: **the pulse envelope** (#1 under Motion hygiene) and **the bidirectional SFX-lettering rule** — the `*thud*` case was the second time the "every sound owes a word" half caught something.

My picks if you only want a few: **1, 2, 10, 11, 17, 21, 27, 28, 31, 39**. Those are the ones that cost the most time and would have been prevented by a written rule.

## USER
can you now tell me the major rules not the minor ones? i couldn't understand 90% of these

## ASSISTANT
That list was written for a build log, not for you. Here are the ones that actually matter, in plain terms.

**1. Move things, don't resize them.**
When something grows, shrinks or pulses, scale it — don't change its actual width and height. Changing real size makes the renderer redraw the edges each frame, and they land a pixel differently every time. That's what the arrow flicker was. It only showed up in the export, which is why it looked like an export problem.

**2. A sound being in the video doesn't mean anyone can hear it.**
The thud was there, on the right frame, at a reasonable level, and inaudible — first because a voice was talking over it in the same pitch range, then because phone speakers can't play notes that low. Always check a sound in the finished cut, not in the file.

**3. Animate the point you're making, not the result.**
The explainer said the right words but gave the biggest movement to things finishing at once. So that's what people took away. Harshit's note wasn't that the explanation was wrong, it's that the loudest thing on screen was the wrong half.

**4. The metaphor has to be the same shape as the idea.**
Doors are about who's allowed through. Dependencies are about what has to happen first. A door can show someone being stopped, but it can't show one thing waiting on another — so the picture quietly taught a different lesson than the words. `WAITING ON API` instead of `PLEASE WAIT` fixes it because it names what's being waited on.

**5. Don't trust a file that says it's already handled. Check the actual audio.**
Twice this bit us. The printer list said 19 clicks; the printer is really a ten-second run, so it stayed in every cut you've seen. And the ElevenLabs take looked right but said "at the same time" instead of "one at a time". Both were fine on paper.

**6. A loop isn't a repeat — something has to carry across the cut.**
The old ending stopped and started again. Now Ken's sentence breaks on "but-" and frame 1 finishes it, on the same picture. That's the difference between looping and restarting, and it's what Anandhi couldn't see before.

**7. Anything baked into a file is only correct for the length it was baked at.**
The office bed is one long pre-made track. Every time the cut got longer it stopped matching, and at one point the last six seconds had no sound at all. Rule: if the timing changes, rebuild it.

**8. Give the character something they want in the first few seconds.**
The deadline used to be a line he agreed to. Now it's something he asks for. That one change is what makes the night, the AI's "you could've been home by eight", and the final question all land — it's the fix for both Prashasti's and Anandhi's notes.

If you only put three in the skill file: **1, 2 and 5.** Those are the ones that cost real time and would have been caught instantly by a written rule.

## ASSISTANT
Yes, connected — `workloom-dev/product-brain`. Skills updated and up as **[PR #578](https://github.com/workloom-dev/product-brain/pull/578)** (not merged, as always).

I had to branch fresh: PR #543 on `docs/comics-graph-intro-scripts` was already merged, so this went on `docs/comics-skill-rules-ep002`.

**What went where**, written in plain terms inside the files rather than as a list of measurements:

**`comic-animation.md`** — the flicker rule (move things, don't resize them, plus how to tell a flicker from an encoding artefact), the pulse-rate ceiling, and how things should enter a scene. Then four explainer rules: the metaphor has to be the same shape as the idea, animate the point rather than the result, turn one picture into the other in place, and don't spend the explanation in the setup.

**`comic-audio.md`** — two new sections. **"Present is not audible"** covers both ways the thud failed: masked by a voice in its own register, then pitched below what phones can play. **"Transcribe every take before you use it"** covers the take that said "at the same time", including the control-clip test for when a transcript is disputed. Plus four ambience rules: baked files go stale, re-derive the noise map from the audio, repeats belong in the silence, and how to make a bed loop.

**`comic-script-writer.md`** — two rules into sections that already existed: a repeat is not a loop (into "The hard cut back to panel 1"), and say the want out loud as a request (into "Somebody has to want something").

**`graph.md`** also went in, since it was already modified — panel 33, the loop reasoning, and the caption and pause decisions.

I left `comic-panel-generator.md` alone, and I didn't touch the two rules this episode only re-confirmed: the pulse envelope and the bidirectional SFX-lettering rule.

The PR body has a table per file so you can see each rule and what it prevents without opening the diff. If any of them read too technical, tell me which and I'll rewrite them the way I wrote the eight-rule version.

## ASSISTANT
Fixed — **[PR #578](https://github.com/workloom-dev/product-brain/pull/578)** is `MERGEABLE / CLEAN`.

**The cause wasn't a real disagreement.** PR #543 was **squash-merged**, and it squashed the branch as it stood at that moment — which was before the script rewrite. So master ended up with the **old 21-panel draft** (Dev 3 / Dev 4, no deadline, no explainer, boxes-and-arrows), while my branch carried the current 33-panel one. Git saw two unrelated versions of the same file and called it an add/add conflict.

Only `graph.md` conflicted. The three skill files merged cleanly.

**How I resolved it:** took my version, since it supersedes master's on every point — same cast renamed, same notes carried forward, plus the deadline, the doors explainer and the loop. Master's panel numbers no longer refer to anything that exists.

**But one note on master was worth keeping**, and it's the only thing I didn't want silently dropped: Mohit's point that the AI *asks* about dependencies and gets waved off, which is what makes the all-nighter AJ's fault and keeps the machine blameless. My version had lost it. I've folded it back in with corrected panel numbers (asks in 11, waved off in 12, pays off in 25) and tied it to why we didn't take the proposed opening where the AI explains parallelism up front — same principle, now recorded in the episode doc as well as the skill file.

Final state: 4 files, +417 / −48, no conflicts. Still not merged.

Worth knowing for next time: if PRs on this repo squash-merge, any commit you push to a branch *after* the PR is opened won't reach master. Commit `1453767` ("adopt Mohit's revised graph engineering script") was lost that way — it only exists now because this branch carried it forward.

---

**Channel:** AI FOMO (@aifomolab) · series *"I Have No Idea What That Means"* · 9x16, ~90–100s per episode. Each episode takes one AI term and explains it through a comic. Zero product content on the channel.

**Cast:** **AJ** the intern (voice: Kai) · **Ken** the senior (voice: Thomas) · **the AI** (voice: David). All VO is generated in ElevenLabs by Sejal.

**Where things live**
- Build repo: `~/Documents/comics-video` — Remotion 4, `src/GraphV1.tsx` is the scene table, `v10/*.py` are the audio/ambience tools. Not a git repo.
- Docs repo: `~/Documents/product-brain` (`workloom-dev/product-brain`) — scripts in `video/youtube/comics/scripts/`, **skills in `video/youtube/comics/skills/`** (`comic-script-writer`, `comic-panel-generator`, `comic-animation`, `comic-audio`). Read all four before starting; they hold every rule learned so far.
- Drive: `01 Scripts/prompts` · `02 Storyboards` · `03 Audio` · `04 Cuts` · `SFX` · `Characters` · `AI mark`. Each has an `EP00N - NAME` subfolder.

**Standing rules that don't live in the skill files**
- No em dashes in any on-screen text. Hyphens in compound words are fine.
- Never rotate or tilt anything; never overlap UI elements. One granted exception: the crooked CLOSED stamp.
- Captions stay fully inside the clean/white area of the plate.
- Character art is generated in ChatGPT only, never Higgsfield.
- PRs may be opened without asking, but **never merge to master**. Conventional Commits title + full description.
- The Drive connector cannot upload images or audio (inline base64 only) — stage files locally and Sejal drags them. Folders and text docs can be created directly.

**EP002 `graph engineering` — done**
Final cut: `out/EP002 graph engineering — 9x16 — 98s (Ken loop, printer-free bed).mp4`, 97.8s. Loops by having Ken's sentence break on "but-" and finish on frame 1.
- Skill-file updates from this episode: [PR #578](https://github.com/workloom-dev/product-brain/pull/578), open, mergeable.
- Still to do by hand: drag the cut into `04 Cuts` (replacing the older upload via **Manage versions**), and drag `out/DRIVE UPLOAD/` into the new `EP002` storyboard and audio folders.

**The two hardest lessons from EP002**, both worth carrying in:
1. **Animate transforms, never box dimensions** — resizing a box re-snaps its edges each frame and flickers only in the export.
2. **Re-derive from the source; don't trust a file that says something is handled** — a stored "printer removed" map and a delivered VO take were both wrong, and both shipped for weeks.

**Open threads:** Mohit hasn't signed off panels 10 and 25; Prashasti's note about committing to one punchline is only partly addressed; the rejected boxes-and-arrows Figma frames (EXP R0–T3) are still sitting on the `graph` page and can be deleted.

## ASSISTANT
Two different jobs here — one's a compliment, one's a real story with a direct question. The AI's register is **deadpan and literal**, not snarky: it never insults anyone, it just restates the facts until they're funny. Its hardest line in the whole episode is "You said 'whatever.'" Keep it sentence case with full stops — that's how the AI reads on screen, and it's how it should read in comments too. No emoji, no exclamation marks.

**To d1nz.3** — this is the one worth spending effort on. He asked a direct question, told a story, and landed on the same fix the episode is about.

> All four. Finished, correct, one at a time. You started saying the dates out loud. He still says "whatever."

That answers him, stays in character, and quietly tells him he already solved it — which is the most flattering thing you can say to someone who shared a war story.

Alternates:

> All four, done and correct. The order was the only thing nobody specified. You learned to say it out loud. He learned nothing.

> All four. On time, by the only definition of time anyone gave me.

**To aakashmondal01:**

> Thank you. Eight hours of it were avoidable.

Alternates:

> Thank you. I did the work. He took the all-nighter.

> Noted. Took eight hours. Could have been four.

I'd go with the first of each. The d1nz.3 reply does real work — he wrote a proper comment, and answering the actual question ("did urs at least finish the four?") with a straight "all four" before the joke is what makes it feel like a reply rather than a bit.

One thing worth deciding now, since it'll come up every episode: **the AI answering in comments is a format, and formats need a rule.** Mine would be that the AI only replies when someone asks it something or tells it about their own AI — the two comments above both qualify. If it replies to everything, it stops being a character and becomes a gimmick. Worth writing into the skill file if you like how these land.
